This cut belongs to the chosen family; take it as $a_j$ and choose the accompanying rational $\gamma_j$ small. Then $x_j=(a_j-\gamma_j)_+$ is as close to $a'$ as desired.

The map $\Psi$ is a positive weight times the homomorphism $\pi$, and the weight commutes with $\pi(A)$. Consequently $$a_j\in\operatorname{Ideal}_A(a')
 \quad\Longrightarrow\quad
 \Psi(a_j)\in\operatorname{Ideal}_B(\Psi(a')):$$ apply $\Psi$ to finite sums of products through $a'$ and pass to norm limits. The witness identity places $e\widehat\theta(x_j)$ in this ideal. The comparison <a href="#trace:small-part-comparison" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="trace:small-part-comparison">[trace:small-part-comparison]</a> similarly places $e\pi(x_j)$ there. Taking norm limits, and then scaling, proves <a href="#support:ideals" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="support:ideals">[support:ideals]</a> for every $a'\in A_+$. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-18"></a>
#### Comparison inside the supported relative commutant

<a id="comm:definition"></a>
<a id="comm:common-equivalence"></a>
Define the c.p.c. order-zero map $$\rho=\operatorname{diag}(\rho_0,\rho_1)\colon A\longrightarrow M_2(B),
 \qquad \rho_0=\pi,\quad\rho_1=\widehat\theta,$$ and its supported relative commutant $$\label{comm:definition}
 C=M_2(B)\cap\rho(A)'\cap\{1-\rho(1_A)\}^{\perp}.$$ The last condition means annihilation on both sides. A matrix in $C$ supported in position $(0,1)$ has an entry that intertwines $\rho_1$ with $\rho_0$ and satisfies both support conditions. The matrix’s two modulus squares lie in the diagonal corners of $C$ and have the same Cuntz class, allowing comparison of positive elements in the two corners inside one semigroup. For $b\in B$, write $b^{(i)}$ for the matrix with entry $b$ in position $(i,i)$ and zeros elsewhere, using indices $i=0,1$. Put $$C_i=\{b\in B:b^{(i)}\in C\},\qquad
 Q_i=q^{(i)},\qquad H=1_B^{(0)}.$$ The common-part identities show that $Q_i,H\in C$. They also imply that the matrix with $q^{1/2}$ in position $(0,1)$ belongs to $C$: positive-support functional calculus passes the intertwining and support identities from $q$ to $q^{1/2}$. Its two modulus squares are $Q_1$ and $Q_0$. Thus $$\label{comm:common-equivalence}
 [Q_0]=[Q_1]\quad\text{in }W(C).$$

<a id="stmt-comm-packing"></a>
<div id="comm:packing" class="proposition">

<a id="comm:small-part"></a>
<a id="comm:two-common"></a>
**Proposition 27** (Packing the error part). *For $i\in\{0,1\}$ and every positive contraction $c\in C_i$, $$\begin{aligned}
 m[(c^{1/2}ec^{1/2})^{(i)}]&\leq[Q_0],
       &&m\in\mathbb N,\label{comm:small-part}\\
 [c^{(i)}]&\leq2[Q_0]
       &&\text{in }W(C).\label{comm:two-common}
\end{aligned}$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-comm-packing"><summary>Proof of stmt-comm-packing</summary>

<a id="comm:supported-product"></a>
*Proof.* Fix $m\geq1$ and set $t=c^{1/2}ec^{1/2}$. This positive contraction commutes with $\rho_i(A)$ and satisfies $t\rho_i(1_A)=t$. The order-zero product identity therefore gives $$\label{comm:supported-product}
 t\rho_i(a)\rho_i(b)=t\rho_i(ab),\qquad a,b\in A.$$ Apply Theorem <a href="#prelim:domination" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prelim:domination">13</a> to $$\Phi(a)=t\rho_i(a),\qquad
 \Psi(a)=P(q-1/2)_+\pi(a).$$ Both maps are completely positive and nuclear, since their domain is nuclear, and $\Psi$ is order zero. For $a\in A_+$, $$\Phi(a)=c^{1/2}e\rho_i(a)c^{1/2}
       \in\operatorname{Ideal}_B(\Psi(a))$$ by Proposition <a href="#support:map" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="support:map">26</a>. Hence finite sums $\sum_{l=1}^L y_l^*\Psi(\,\cdot\,)y_l$ approximate $\Phi$ pointwise in norm.

<a id="comm:packed-approximation"></a>
Write $h_q=(q-1/2)_+$. For one such approximation, take $mL$ second-leg elements $v_{sl}$ from <a href="#trace:ultrapower-copies" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="trace:ultrapower-copies">[trace:ultrapower-copies]</a> and set $$r_s=\sum_{l=1}^L v_{sl}h_q^{1/2}y_l,
 \qquad 1\leq s\leq m.$$ These second-leg elements commute with $q$ and $\pi(A)$, and satisfy $v_{sl}^*v_{s'l'}=\delta_{ss'}\delta_{ll'}P$. Expanding the products therefore gives $$\label{comm:packed-approximation}
 r_s^*\pi(a)r_{s'}=
 \delta_{ss'}\sum_{l=1}^L y_l^*\Psi(a)y_l,
 \qquad a\in A.$$ For $f(u)=\min\{1,2u\}$ on $[0,1]$ one also has $f(q)r_s=r_s$, since $f(q)h_q^{1/2}=h_q^{1/2}$. Include $1_A$ in the approximation set. As $\Phi(1_A)=t$ and $\|t\|\leq1$, sufficiently accurate approximations in <a href="#comm:packed-approximation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="comm:packed-approximation">[comm:packed-approximation]</a> have $\|r_s\|^2\leq2$. Thus, for this fixed $m$, there is a fixed bound on every packed variable, independent of $L$ and of the approximation accuracy.

<a id="comm:packed-exact"></a>
Using bounded coordinate lifts and the diagonalization principle of Lemma <a href="#prelim:epsilon-test" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prelim:epsilon-test">11</a> on a countable dense subset of $A$, we obtain $r_1,\ldots,r_m\in B$ with $$\label{comm:packed-exact}
 f(q)r_s=r_s,\qquad
 r_s^*\pi(a)r_{s'}=\delta_{ss'}t\rho_i(a),
 \qquad a\in A.$$ These elements are intertwiners. Indeed, using <a href="#comm:supported-product" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="comm:supported-product">[comm:supported-product]</a> and <a href="#comm:packed-exact" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="comm:packed-exact">[comm:packed-exact]</a>, the right modulus square of $\pi(a)r_s-r_s\rho_i(a)$ is $$t\rho_i(a^*a)-t\rho_i(a^*)\rho_i(a)=0.$$ Consequently $\pi(a)r_s=r_s\rho_i(a)$ for all $a\in A$. Taking $a=1_A$ gives $r_s\rho_i(1_A)=r_s$ as well. The matrix $R_s$ with $r_s$ in position $(0,i)$ thus belongs to $C$, including its support condition.

For the row $V=(R_1,\ldots,R_m)$ over $C$, the positive matrix $V^*V$ has $m$ diagonal entries equal to $t^{(i)}$ and all other entries zero. Meanwhile $VV^*\in\operatorname{her}_C(Q_0)$: <a href="#comm:packed-exact" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="comm:packed-exact">[comm:packed-exact]</a> gives $f(Q_0)VV^*f(Q_0)=VV^*$, and $f(0)=0$. Equality of the Cuntz classes of the two modulus squares now proves <a href="#comm:small-part" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="comm:small-part">[comm:small-part]</a>.

Finally, $$c=c^{1/2}ec^{1/2}+c^{1/2}qc^{1/2}.$$ The second summand, placed in corner $i$, is Cuntz below $Q_i$ inside $C$. Apply <a href="#comm:small-part" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="comm:small-part">[comm:small-part]</a> with $m=1$, the subadditivity of Cuntz classes of positive sums, and <a href="#comm:common-equivalence" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="comm:common-equivalence">[comm:common-equivalence]</a>. This proves <a href="#comm:two-common" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="comm:two-common">[comm:two-common]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-comm-fullness"></a>
<div id="comm:fullness" class="corollary">

**Corollary 28**. *The elements $Q_0,Q_1$ and $H$ are full in $C$, and $C$ is full in $M_2(B)$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-comm-fullness"><summary>Proof of stmt-comm-fullness</summary>

*Proof.* Every positive element of $M_2(B)$ is bounded by twice its diagonal part. For an element of $C_+$, each diagonal corner also lies in $C$, because $\rho$ and its support condition are diagonal. After scaling, <a href="#comm:two-common" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="comm:two-common">[comm:two-common]</a> shows that these corners belong to the ideal generated by $Q_0$. Hence so does the original positive element. Thus $Q_0$ is full; the same holds for $Q_1$ by <a href="#comm:common-equivalence" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="comm:common-equivalence">[comm:common-equivalence]</a>, and for $H$ because $Q_0\leq H$. The corner projection $H$ is full in the ambient matrix algebra $M_2(B)$, so the latter also contains $C$ as a full subalgebra. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-comm-unperforated"></a>
<div id="comm:unperforated" class="proposition">

**Proposition 29**. *The ordered semigroup $W(C)$ is almost unperforated.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-comm-unperforated"><summary>Proof of stmt-comm-unperforated</summary>

*Proof.* Suppose $(N+1)x\leq Ny$ in $W(C)$, with $N\geq1$. Choose a separable $C^*$-subalgebra $C_*\subset C$ containing the matrix entries of representatives of $x,y$ and of countably many comparison witnesses. The inequality then already holds over $C_*$.

There is a unital copy of $\mathcal Z$ in $M_2(B)$ commuting with both $C_*$ and $\rho(A)$. Here is the coordinate argument, which also allows the representatives of $C_*$ to vary with the coordinate. Choose isomorphisms $\sigma_l\colon\mathcal Z\otimes\mathcal Z\to\mathcal Z$ with $\sigma_l(d\otimes1)\to d$, and put $\nu_l(z)=\sigma_l(1\otimes z)$. For fixed $d\in\mathcal Z$, $$\|[\nu_l(z),d]\|
 \leq 2\|z\|\,\|d-\sigma_l(d\otimes1)\|.$$ Thus the commutators tend to zero uniformly for $z$ in the unit ball. Place these embeddings diagonally on the third tensor leg of $M_2(R)$. Tensor approximation gives the same uniform commutation property with any fixed element of $M_2(R)$. At coordinate $n$, choose $l$ sufficiently large for the entries of representatives of the first $n$ elements of countable dense commutation data, with tolerance tending to zero. The resulting coordinate embeddings induce a unital embedding $\nu\colon\mathcal Z\to M_2(B)$ commuting with $C_*$ and $\rho(A)$. It is isometric because each coordinate embedding is isometric.

Multiplication gives a homomorphism $$C_*\otimes\mathcal Z\longrightarrow C,\qquad c\otimes z\longmapsto c\nu(z).$$ Commutation gives a homomorphism on the maximal tensor product; nuclearity of $\mathcal Z$ identifies it with the spatial tensor product. The range satisfies the support condition because $c$ does and $\nu(z)$ commutes with $\rho(1_A)$. This homomorphism restricts to the original inclusion on $C_*\otimes1$.

The algebra $C_*\otimes\mathcal Z$ is $\mathcal Z$-stable. Its Cuntz semigroup is therefore almost unperforated by Theorem <a href="#prelim:comparison" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prelim:comparison">8</a>. Tensoring the original inequality with $1_\mathcal Z$ gives comparison of the images of $x$ and $y$ there. Mapping the comparison back to $C$ yields $x\leq y$.

Here the reindexed copy $\nu(\mathcal Z)$ is used for this inequality alone. The constant third-leg copy retained above is the separate source of the weight $k$ below; no commutation between these two copies is required. ◻

</details>

We next use the full projection $H$, the corner divisibility supplied by Lemma <a href="#supplies:copies" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="supplies:copies">21</a>, and almost unperforation of $W(C)$ to verify the structural hereditary hypothesis of the BBSTWW criterion recalled in Lemma <a href="#prelim:unitary" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prelim:unitary">14</a>.

<a id="stmt-conjugacy-hereditary"></a>
<div id="conjugacy:hereditary" class="proposition">

**Proposition 30**. *Let $D\subset C$ be a full hereditary subalgebra. Suppose that $y\in D$ and that totally full positive elements $e_l,e_r\in D$ satisfy $$e_l y=ye_r=0.$$ Then there is a full element $s\in D$ with $sy=ys=0$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-conjugacy-hereditary"><summary>Proof of stmt-conjugacy-hereditary</summary>

<a id="conjugacy:projection-domination"></a>
*Proof.* Fullness of $e_l,e_r$ in $D$ and fullness of $D$ in $C$ imply that both elements are full in $C$. Since $H$ is a projection, there is a common finite integer $N\geq1$ such that $$\label{conjugacy:projection-domination}
 [H]\leq N[e_l],\qquad [H]\leq N[e_r]
 \quad\text{in }W(C).$$ Use the positive contraction $D_{N+1}$ from the order-zero supplies on a constant $\mathcal Z$ leg, and put $p_0=(D_{N+1})^{(0)}$. It belongs to $C$, since the first-corner map is $\pi$, and it has norm one. Simplicity of the constant leg gives fullness of $p_0$ in the corner with unit $H$; because $H$ is full in $C$, so is $p_0$.

The $N+1$ accompanying elements from Lemma <a href="#supplies:copies" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="supplies:copies">21</a>, placed in this corner, form a row over $C$ whose right modulus square is the diagonal sum of $N+1$ copies of $p_0$, while its left modulus square belongs to $HCH$. Hence $$(N+1)[p_0]\leq[H].$$ Combining this with <a href="#conjugacy:projection-domination" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="conjugacy:projection-domination">[conjugacy:projection-domination]</a> and Proposition <a href="#comm:unperforated" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="comm:unperforated">29</a> gives $p_0\precsim e_l,e_r$ in $C$. We may therefore choose $w_l,w_r\in C$ so that $$A_l=w_l^*e_lw_l,\qquad A_r=w_r^*e_rw_r$$ are as close to $p_0$ as prescribed.

<a id="conjugacy:fullness-tolerance"></a>
We specify the accuracy before choosing these witnesses. Fullness of $p_0^2$ in the constant simple leg gives fixed $z_1,\ldots,z_L\in HCH$ with $$\sum_{j=1}^L z_j^*p_0^2z_j=H.$$ Choose $\varepsilon>0$ such that $$\label{conjugacy:fullness-tolerance}
 (2\varepsilon+\varepsilon^2)
       \sum_{j=1}^L\|z_j\|^2<1,$$ and choose $w_l,w_r$ with $\|A_l-p_0\|,\|A_r-p_0\|<\varepsilon$. Set $$s=e_r^{1/2}w_rw_l^*e_l^{1/2}.$$ Heredity gives $s\in D$, since $DCD\subset D$. Functional calculus applied to the annihilation identities gives $e_l^{1/2}y=ye_r^{1/2}=0$, so $sy=ys=0$.

Let $J=\operatorname{Ideal}_C(s)$. It contains $$w_r^*e_r^{1/2}s\,e_l^{1/2}w_l=A_rA_l,
 \qquad
 \|A_rA_l-p_0^2\|\leq2\varepsilon+\varepsilon^2.$$ Thus the element $b=\sum_j z_j^*A_rA_lz_j\in J$ satisfies $\|H-b\|<1$ by <a href="#conjugacy:fullness-tolerance" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="conjugacy:fullness-tolerance">[conjugacy:fullness-tolerance]</a>. The image of $H$ in $C/J$ is a projection of norm less than one, and is therefore zero. Since $H$ is full in $C$, this forces $J=C$.

It remains to prove that $s$ is full inside $D$. Let $I=\operatorname{Ideal}_D(s)$. For any ideal $I$ of a hereditary subalgebra $D\subset C$, one has $$D\,\operatorname{Ideal}_C(I)\,D\subset I.$$ Indeed, the ideal in the middle is the closed linear span of products through elements of $I$. By linear decomposition it is enough to use a positive middle element $i\in I_+$, and then $$dci c'd'
   =(dc\,i^{1/3})\,i^{1/3}\,(i^{1/3}c'd')\in I:$$ the two outer factors lie in $D$ by heredity. Here $\operatorname{Ideal}_C(I)$ contains $J=C$, so the displayed inclusion and an approximate identity of $D$ give $D\subset I$. Hence $I=D$, proving that $s$ is full in $D$. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-19"></a>
#### Full-spectrum weights and unitary equivalence

The structural conditions on $C$ in Lemma <a href="#prelim:unitary" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prelim:unitary">14</a> are now verified. We turn to the remaining input: totally full contractions with matching Cuntz classes under positive functional calculus.

<a id="conjugacy:weights"></a>
Fix a positive contraction $k$ of spectrum $[0,1]$ in the retained constant third leg. Such a $k$ is obtained from the scalar interval coordinate in a prime dimension-drop algebra embedded in $\mathcal Z_3$. When necessary, a third-leg element also denotes its diagonal copy in $M_2(B)$. Set $$\label{conjugacy:weights}
 h_0=k^{(0)},\qquad h_1=(kh)^{(1)}.$$ These are positive contractions in $C$. In the second corner, the support condition follows from $h\widehat\theta(1_A)=h$; commutation follows from Proposition <a href="#support:map" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="support:map">26</a>.

<a id="stmt-conjugacy-functions"></a>
<div id="conjugacy:functions" class="proposition">

**Proposition 31**. *The elements $h_0,h_1$ are totally full in $C$. For every $g\in C_0((0,1])_+$, the elements $g(h_0)$ and $g(h_1)$ are Cuntz equivalent in $C$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-conjugacy-functions"><summary>Proof of stmt-conjugacy-functions</summary>

<a id="conjugacy:common-functions"></a>
*Proof.* The case $g=0$ is immediate. Otherwise rescale so that $\|g\|\leq1$, and put $$G_i=g(h_i),\qquad T_i=Q_iG_i,\qquad E_i=G_i-T_i=e^{(i)}G_i.$$ All these elements are positive and belong to $C$. The factors commute because $e$ commutes with $h$ and is represented in the first subsystem. Since $qh=q$, functional calculus gives $T_i=Q_i g(k)$. The matrix with $q^{1/2}g(k)^{1/2}$ in position $(0,1)$ belongs to $C$ and has modulus squares $T_1,T_0$. Consequently $$\label{conjugacy:common-functions}
 [T_0]=[T_1]\quad\text{in }W(C).$$

<a id="conjugacy:fullness-bound"></a>
The positive element $g(k)$ is nonzero in the simple unital constant third leg. Choose finitely many elements $z_1,\ldots,z_K$ in that leg with $$\sum_{l=1}^K z_l^*g(k)z_l=1.$$ For $\delta>0$ let $$b_\delta(u)=
 \begin{cases}
 ((u-\delta)_+/u)^{1/2},&u>0,\\
 0,&u=0.
 \end{cases}$$ The diagonalized $z_l$ commute with $Q_i$ and $\rho(A)$ and multiply $C$ into itself. In particular $z_l b_\delta(Q_i)\in C$, and $$(Q_i-\delta)_+
 =\sum_{l=1}^K
   (z_l b_\delta(Q_i))^*T_i(z_l b_\delta(Q_i)).$$ Taking all cuts proves $$\label{conjugacy:fullness-bound}
 [Q_i]\leq K[T_i]\leq K[G_i]\quad\text{in }W(C).$$ Since $Q_i$ is full, this shows that $g(h_i)$ is full for every nonzero $g$. In particular the two elements in <a href="#conjugacy:weights" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="conjugacy:weights">[conjugacy:weights]</a> are totally full; the same argument also ensures that every such functional calculus is nonzero.

<a id="conjugacy:dimension-equality"></a>
Let $d$ be any dimension function on $W(C)$ normalized at either $[G_0]$ or $[G_1]$, if one exists. Equations <a href="#comm:two-common" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="comm:two-common">[comm:two-common]</a> and <a href="#conjugacy:fullness-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="conjugacy:fullness-bound">[conjugacy:fullness-bound]</a>, together with <a href="#comm:common-equivalence" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="comm:common-equivalence">[comm:common-equivalence]</a>, imply $$0<d([Q_0])=d([Q_1])<\infty.$$ Apply <a href="#comm:small-part" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="comm:small-part">[comm:small-part]</a> with $c^{(i)}=G_i$. Since $G_i$ commutes with $e^{(i)}$, it follows that $m[E_i]\leq[Q_0]$ for every $m$, and hence $d([E_i])=0$. The inequalities $$[T_i]\leq[G_i]\leq[T_i]+[E_i]$$ and <a href="#conjugacy:common-functions" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="conjugacy:common-functions">[conjugacy:common-functions]</a> now give $$\label{conjugacy:dimension-equality}
 d([G_0])=d([G_1])=1.$$

Fix $\eta>0$. By continuity, $g(0)=0$, and $g\ne0$, there is a nonzero continuous function $g'$ with $0\leq g'\leq g$ and support contained in the open set where $0<g<\eta$. For the dimension functions under consideration, <a href="#conjugacy:fullness-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="conjugacy:fullness-bound">[conjugacy:fullness-bound]</a> applied to $g'$ gives $d([g'(h_i)])>0$. This element is orthogonal to $(G_i-\eta)_+$, and their sum is bounded by $G_i$. Therefore <a href="#conjugacy:dimension-equality" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="conjugacy:dimension-equality">[conjugacy:dimension-equality]</a> gives $$d([(G_i-\eta)_+])+d([g'(h_i)])\leq1,
 \qquad
 d([(G_i-\eta)_+])<1=d([G_{1-i}]).$$ This holds for every dimension function normalized at $[G_{1-i}]$. The target $G_{1-i}$ is full, so the ideal condition for the comparison criterion in Theorem <a href="#prelim:comparison" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prelim:comparison">8</a> holds. Proposition <a href="#comm:unperforated" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="comm:unperforated">29</a> and that criterion yield $(G_i-\eta)_+\precsim G_{1-i}$. This use of the criterion also permits an empty family of normalized dimension functions. Taking all positive cuts, and then exchanging $i=0,1$, proves the required Cuntz equivalence. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-conjugacy-weighted"></a>
<div id="conjugacy:weighted" class="proposition">

<a id="conjugacy:weighted-equation"></a>
**Proposition 32** (Weighted unitary equivalence). *Put $k^{[0]}=k$ and $k^{[1]}=1-k$, in the constant third leg. There are unitaries $U_0,U_1\in M_2(B)$ such that $$\label{conjugacy:weighted-equation}
 U_i\bigl(k^{[i]}\theta(a)\bigr)^{(1)}U_i^*
   =\bigl(k^{[i]}\pi(a)\bigr)^{(0)},
 \qquad a\in A,\quad i=0,1.$$ They can be chosen to commute with $\rho(A)$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-conjugacy-weighted"><summary>Proof of stmt-conjugacy-weighted</summary>

*Proof.* Apply the relative-commutant unitary-equivalence criterion of Lemma <a href="#prelim:unitary" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prelim:unitary">14</a>, namely <a id="pm-cite-59"></a>[[BBSTWW](#ref-BBSTWW), Lemma 5.3], to <a href="#comm:definition" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="comm:definition">[comm:definition]</a>. Its coordinate algebras are $M_2(R)$, which are separable, unital and $\mathcal Z$-stable, and its domain is the separable unital algebra $A$. The map $\rho$ is c.p.c. order zero. Corollary <a href="#comm:fullness" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="comm:fullness">28</a> verifies fullness of $C$ in the ambient ultrapower; Proposition <a href="#conjugacy:hereditary" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="conjugacy:hereditary">30</a> verifies the required condition in every full hereditary subalgebra. Proposition <a href="#conjugacy:functions" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="conjugacy:functions">31</a> supplies total fullness and all the functional-calculus comparisons. Thus there is a unitary in the unitization of $C$ conjugating $h_1$ to $h_0$.

Realizing this unitary in the ambient algebra as explained after Lemma <a href="#prelim:unitary" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prelim:unitary">14</a> gives $U_0\in M_2(B)$ commuting with $\rho(A)$. Multiplying $U_0h_1U_0^*=h_0$ by $\rho(a)$ and using <a href="#support:factorization" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="support:factorization">[support:factorization]</a> gives $$U_0(k\theta(a))^{(1)}U_0^*=(k\pi(a))^{(0)}.$$ The element $1-k$ also has spectrum $[0,1]$ in the constant third leg. Repeating the same argument with this element, with all the previously constructed maps unchanged, gives $U_1$ and the other identity in <a href="#conjugacy:weighted-equation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="conjugacy:weighted-equation">[conjugacy:weighted-equation]</a>. ◻

</details>

Complementary weights are part of the two-coloured approximation framework developed in <a id="pm-cite-60"></a>[[SWW](#ref-SWW), [BBSTWW](#ref-BBSTWW), [CETWW](#ref-CETWW)]. Here the required ideal-sensitive comparisons have been proved above. The two identities in <a href="#conjugacy:weighted-equation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="conjugacy:weighted-equation">[conjugacy:weighted-equation]</a> add to the first-corner inclusion because $k+(1-k)=1$. The next section passes from these ultrapower identities to finite-dimensional approximations with exactly two order-zero return maps.

<a id="sec:endgame"></a>
### Two colours and hereditary permanence

We first turn the ultrapower conjugacies into finite-dimensional approximations of a corner inclusion. We then use stabilization to retain the same two colours when the target is the algebra itself.

<a id="stmt-end-corner"></a>
<div id="end:corner" class="proposition">

**Proposition 33** (Two-colour corner approximation). *Let $A$ be a nonzero separable unital nuclear $C^*$-algebra and put $R=A\otimes\mathcal Z^{\otimes3}$. For every finite $\mathcal F\subset A$ and $\varepsilon>0$, the map $$\kappa_A:A\longrightarrow M_2(R),\qquad
 \kappa_A(a)=\begin{pmatrix}a\otimes1&0\\0&0\end{pmatrix}$$ admits a finite-dimensional approximation with two colours: there are a finite-dimensional algebra $F$, a c.p.c. map $\alpha:A\to F$, and c.p.c. order-zero maps $\varphi_0,\varphi_1:F\to M_2(R)$ such that $$\|\varphi_0\alpha(a)+\varphi_1\alpha(a)-\kappa_A(a)\|
 <\varepsilon\qquad(a\in\mathcal F).$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-end-corner"><summary>Proof of stmt-end-corner</summary>

*Proof.* Use the notation and the two unitaries of Proposition <a href="#conjugacy:weighted" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="conjugacy:weighted">32</a>. Write $k^{[0]}=k$ and $k^{[1]}=1-k$. Each unitary $U_i\in M_2(R_\omega)$ has unitary representatives $U_{i,n}\in M_2(R)$. Indeed, a bounded representative is asymptotically unitary on both sides. On an $\omega$-large set its coordinates are invertible and polar correction gives unitaries with the same quotient class; put the identity on the remaining coordinates.

For the matrix-model maps $\alpha_n:A\to F_n$ and $\beta'_n:F_n\to A\otimes\mathcal Z_1\subset R$, define $$\varphi_{i,n}(b)
 =U_{i,n}\begin{pmatrix}0&0\\0&k^{[i]}\beta'_n(b)\end{pmatrix}U_{i,n}^*.$$ Each map is c.p.c. order zero: its weight is a positive contraction in the third tensor factor, commuting with the range of $\beta'_n$; corner inclusion and unitary conjugation preserve these properties. The conjugacies in Proposition <a href="#conjugacy:weighted" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="conjugacy:weighted">32</a> give, for every $a\in A$, $$\lim_{n\to\omega}
 \|\varphi_{0,n}\alpha_n(a)+\varphi_{1,n}\alpha_n(a)-\kappa_A(a)\|=0,$$ since $k^{[0]}+k^{[1]}=1$. Choose one coordinate in the intersection of the finitely many $\omega$-large approximation sets for $a\in\mathcal F$. This proves the assertion. The first map into $F_n\oplus F_n$ is $a\mapsto(\alpha_n(a),\alpha_n(a))$; it is contractive because the direct-sum norm is the maximum norm. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-auto-remark-1"></a>
<div class="remark">

*Remark 34* (Keeping two colours under stabilization). To pass from the corner inclusion to the algebra itself, we first amplify by finite matrices. Matrix amplification preserves each order-zero return map, and the stabilization accommodates the second diagonal block of the amplified target. The resulting approximations still have two colours.

</div>

<a id="stmt-end-stabilize"></a>
<div id="end:stabilize" class="lemma">

**Lemma 35** (Passing from a corner to the stabilization). *Let $A$ be unital. Suppose that the first-corner inclusion $A\to M_2(A)$ has finite-dimensional approximations with two colours, with a c.p.c. first map and c.p.c. order-zero return maps. Then $\dim_{\mathrm{nuc}}(A\otimes\mathcal K)\le1$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-end-stabilize"><summary>Proof of stmt-end-stabilize</summary>

*Proof.* Fix $m\ge1$. For a finite set of elements of $A\otimes M_m$, choose a two-colour approximation of $A\to M_2(A)$ accurate on all their matrix entries. Amplify every map by $\mathrm{id}_{M_m}$. The intermediate algebra is still the direct sum of two finite-dimensional algebras, namely $E_0\otimes M_m$ and $E_1\otimes M_m$. The first map remains c.p.c. Each amplified return map remains c.p.c. order zero: if $\varphi=h\varphi_{\mathrm{hom}}$, then $$(\varphi\otimes\mathrm{id}_{M_m})(x)
 =(h\otimes1)(\varphi_{\mathrm{hom}}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{M_m})(x),$$ with a commuting positive contractive weight.

Embed $M_2(A)\otimes M_m$ into $A\otimes\mathcal K$ so that its first diagonal $m\times m$ block is the prescribed first $m$-corner. The amplified approximation then approaches the inclusion of $A\otimes M_m$ into $A\otimes\mathcal K$. Entrywise accuracy suffices: an $m\times m$ matrix with entries of norm at most $\delta$ has norm at most $m\delta$.

Let $p_m=1_A\otimes\sum_{r=1}^m e_{rr}$. Precompose the amplified first map with the c.p.c. compression $x\mapsto p_mxp_m$ on $A\otimes\mathcal K$. For an arbitrary prescribed finite set in the stabilization, first choose $m$ so that these compressions approximate every element, then approximate the compressed finite set as above. The resulting composite has the same value on $x$ and $p_mxp_m$, so the total error is bounded by the compression error plus the finite-corner approximation error. This gives the required two-colour approximation on the stabilization. ◻

</details>

<details id="proof-stmt-end-stabilize-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-end-stabilize</summary>

*Proof of Theorem <a href="#intro:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="intro:main">3</a>.* First let $A_0$ be nonzero and unital, and set $A=A_0\otimes\mathcal Z$. Strong self-absorption gives isomorphisms $$\sigma_l:\mathcal Z\otimes\mathcal Z\longrightarrow\mathcal Z,
 \qquad \sigma_l(z\otimes1)\longrightarrow z.$$ Identify the three extra copies of $\mathcal Z$ with one copy by a fixed unital isomorphism. The maps $\mathrm{id}_{A_0}\otimes\sigma_l$ then give isomorphisms $$\Gamma_l:A\otimes\mathcal Z^{\otimes3}\longrightarrow A,
 \qquad \Gamma_l(a\otimes1)\longrightarrow a\quad(a\in A).$$ The convergence holds first on elementary tensors and then on all of $A$ by contractivity.

Given a finite subset of $A$, choose $l$ so that $\Gamma_l$ has the required accuracy on its first-factor images. Apply Proposition <a href="#end:corner" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="end:corner">33</a> with a sufficiently small error, and postcompose its return maps with $\mathrm{id}_{M_2}\otimes\Gamma_l$. This gives two-colour approximations to $A\to M_2(A)$. Lemma <a href="#end:stabilize" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="end:stabilize">35</a> shows that $\dim_{\mathrm{nuc}}(A\otimes\mathcal K)\le1$.

Nuclear dimension decreases on hereditary subalgebras <a id="pm-cite-61"></a>[[WZNuclear](#ref-WZNuclear), Proposition 2.5]. Applying this to the first corner $A\otimes e_{11}$ gives $\dim_{\mathrm{nuc}}(A_0\otimes\mathcal Z)\le1$. For nonunital separable nuclear $A_0$, its unitization $\widetilde A_0$ is separable and nuclear. The unital case applies to $\widetilde A_0\otimes\mathcal Z$, and $A_0\otimes\mathcal Z$ is a closed two-sided ideal, hence a hereditary subalgebra, of that algebra. The same permanence result gives the bound. The zero algebra is immediate.

Finally, if $A_0\cong A_0\otimes\mathcal Z$, transport these finite-dimensional approximations across the isomorphism. The first map is c.p.c., the two return maps are individually c.p.c. order zero, and their sum approximates the identity, exactly as in Definition <a href="#prelim:nuclear-dimension" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prelim:nuclear-dimension">5</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:gaussian"></a>
### A real tensor with uniform sign separation

The dimension bound is now proved. We turn to the independent splitting theorem that will supply absorption from finite nuclear dimension. Its first ingredient is purely real and finite-dimensional: one tensor must take both signs on every sufficiently separated product of two-point sets. The following lemma obtains this simultaneously over all such configurations. We equip real tensor powers with the Euclidean tensor product inner product.

<a id="stmt-lem-gaussian"></a>
<div id="lem:gaussian" class="lemma">

**Lemma 36**. *Fix $k\geq2$ and $0<r<1$. There are $n\geq1$ and $G\in(\mathbb R^k)^{\otimes n}$ such that, for every list of pairs of unit vectors $(u_j^0,u_j^1)_{j=1}^n$ satisfying $$|\langle u_j^0,u_j^1\rangle|\leq r\qquad(1\leq j\leq n),$$ the numbers $$\big\langle G,u_1^{\alpha_1}\otimes\cdots\otimes u_n^{\alpha_n}\big\rangle,
 \qquad \alpha\in\{0,1\}^n,$$ include a value greater than $1/2$ and a value less than $-1/2$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-gaussian"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-gaussian</summary>

*Proof.* Choose $$0<a<\min\left\{\log2-\log(4/3)-\tfrac14\log3,\ -\tfrac14\log r\right\}.$$ The first expression is positive. A binary Hamming ball of radius $\lfloor n/4\rfloor$ has cardinality at most $$\sum_{b=0}^{\lfloor n/4\rfloor}\binom nb
 \leq 3^{n/4}\sum_{b=0}^n\binom nb3^{-b}
 =3^{n/4}(4/3)^n.$$ Gilbert’s greedy deletion argument <a id="pm-cite-62"></a>[[Gilbert](#ref-Gilbert), Theorem 1] therefore produces, for all sufficiently large $n$, a set $S_n\subset\{0,1\}^n$ of cardinality $N=\lfloor e^{an}\rfloor$ whose distinct words have Hamming distance at least $n/4$.

Let the coordinates of $G$ be independent standard real Gaussian variables. Fix an admissible list and set $$X_\alpha=\langle G,u_1^{\alpha_1}\otimes\cdots\otimes u_n^{\alpha_n}\rangle
 \quad(\alpha\in S_n).$$ These variables are jointly centered Gaussian. Their covariance matrix $\Sigma$ has diagonal entries one and off-diagonal entries of absolute value at most $r^{n/4}$. Consequently its eigenvalues lie in $[1-\lambda_n,1+\lambda_n]$, where $$\lambda_n=N r^{n/4}\longrightarrow0.$$ For large $n$, the density of this Gaussian vector is bounded pointwise by $$\left(\frac{1+\lambda_n}{1-\lambda_n}\right)^{N/2}$$ times the density of $N$ independent centered Gaussians of variance $1+\lambda_n$. Indeed, $\det\Sigma\geq(1-\lambda_n)^N$ and $\Sigma^{-1}\geq(1+\lambda_n)^{-1}I$.

<a id="eq:prob"></a>
Writing $\Phi$ for the standard normal distribution function gives $$\label{eq:prob}
 \Pr(X_\alpha\leq1\ \forall\alpha\in S_n)
 \leq\left[\sqrt{\frac{1+\lambda_n}{1-\lambda_n}}\,
 \Phi\big((1+\lambda_n)^{-1/2}\big)\right]^N
 \leq q^N$$ for one $q<1$ and every sufficiently large $n$. The last inequality is uniform over admissible lists because the bracket tends to $\Phi(1)<1$. By symmetry the same bound holds for the event $X_\alpha\geq-1$ for all $\alpha$.

Set $\eta_n=(4n k^{n/2})^{-1}$. The compact space of admissible lists has an $\eta_n$-net, in the maximum Euclidean distance between corresponding vectors, of size at most $$(C_k/\eta_n)^{2kn}.$$ For example, mesh the coordinate cubes and select an admissible representative from each occupied box. The logarithm of this bound is $O(n^2)$. A union bound using <a href="#eq:prob" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:prob">[eq:prob]</a> shows that the probability that some net list lacks a value above $1$ or below $-1$ is at most $$2(C_k/\eta_n)^{2kn}q^N\longrightarrow0.$$ Also $\mathbb E\|G\|^2=k^n$, so $\Pr(\|G\|\leq2k^{n/2})\geq3/4$. For large $n$, both the successful-net event and this norm bound therefore hold for some $G$.

If an admissible list is approximated by a net list, telescoping the elementary tensors of unit vectors bounds the change of each evaluation by $$\|G\|\,n\eta_n\leq\tfrac12.$$ The two evaluations exceeding $1$ and below $-1$ on the net list retain values above $1/2$ and below $-1/2$, respectively. This proves the assertion for every admissible list. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:tensor"></a>
### Testing signs in maximal tensor products

The goal is to prove Theorem <a href="#thm:tensor-intro" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:tensor-intro">4</a>: the two signs of one selfadjoint element will survive in every irreducible representation. Such representations of a maximal tensor product need not be spatial. The following factor argument and the pure-state excision method of Akemann–Anderson–Pedersen <a id="pm-cite-63"></a>[[AAP](#ref-AAP), Proposition 2.2] let us transfer the real evaluations of Lemma <a href="#lem:gaussian" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:gaussian">36</a> without a spatial representation hypothesis. We include the required excision proof.

<a id="stmt-lem-factors"></a>
<div id="lem:factors" class="lemma">

**Lemma 37** (Products in commuting factors). *Let $M_1,\ldots,M_n\subseteq\mathcal B(H)$ be mutually commuting unital von Neumann factors. If $e_j\in M_j$ is positive and $\|e_j\|=1$, then $$\left\|\prod_{j=1}^ne_j\right\|=1.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-factors"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-factors</summary>

*Proof.* First, a product of nonzero projections $p_j\in M_j$ is nonzero. Induct on $n$. By induction $q=p_2\cdots p_n$ is a nonzero projection commuting with $M_1$. The restriction $M_1\to\mathcal B(qH)$ is a nonzero normal representation. Its kernel is a weak-\* closed ideal in a factor and is therefore zero. Thus $p_1q\ne0$.

For $0<\varepsilon<1$, the spectral projections $p_j=\mathbf1_{(1-\varepsilon,1]}(e_j)$ are nonzero. Their product is nonzero, and on its range the positive product $\prod_j e_j$ is at least $(1-\varepsilon)^n$. Letting $\varepsilon\downarrow0$ proves the lower bound one; the reverse bound follows from contractivity. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-lem-excision"></a>
<div id="lem:excision" class="lemma">

**Lemma 38** (Pure-state excision). *Let $E$ be a unital $C^*$-algebra and $\rho$ a pure state. Given a finite set $\mathcal F\subset E$ and $\varepsilon>0$, there is a positive contraction $f\in E$ with $\|f\|=1$ such that $$\|fxf-\rho(x)f^2\|<\varepsilon\qquad(x\in\mathcal F).$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-excision"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-excision</summary>

*Proof.* Let $(\gamma,H,\xi)$ be the irreducible GNS representation of $\rho$, with $\|\xi\|=1$, and put $$L=\{z\in E:\gamma(z)\xi=0\}.$$ We first show that $\ker\rho=L+L^*$. The inclusion from right to left is immediate. If $\rho(x)=0$, write $\eta=\gamma(x)\xi\perp\xi$. If $\eta=0$, then $x\in L$. Otherwise, on $\operatorname{span}\{\xi,\eta\}$ let $T$ be the rank-one operator with $T\xi=\eta$ and $T\eta=0$; thus $T^*\xi=0$. Apply the selfadjoint form of Kadison transitivity <a id="pm-cite-64"></a>[[Kadison](#ref-Kadison), Theorem 1] separately to the real and imaginary parts of $T$. This gives $y\in E$ such that $$\gamma(y)\xi=\eta,\qquad\gamma(y^*)\xi=0.$$ Then $x-y\in L$ and $y\in L^*$, as desired.

The algebra $L\cap L^*$ is hereditary. Let $(g_\lambda)$ be a positive contractive approximate identity for it. If $z\in L$, then $z^*z\in L\cap L^*$, whence $$\|z(1-g_\lambda)\|^2
 =\|(1-g_\lambda)z^*z(1-g_\lambda)\|\longrightarrow0.$$ The adjoint assertion holds for elements of $L^*$. Therefore, with $f_\lambda=1-g_\lambda$, the equality $\ker\rho=L+L^*$ gives $$\|f_\lambda(x-\rho(x)1)f_\lambda\|\longrightarrow0
 \qquad(x\in E).$$ Each $f_\lambda$ is a positive contraction of norm one because $\gamma(f_\lambda)\xi=\xi$. One sufficiently large $\lambda$ works for $\mathcal F$. ◻

</details>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-excision-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-excision</summary>

<a id="eq:commutator"></a>
*Proof of Theorem <a href="#thm:tensor-intro" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:tensor-intro">4</a>.* The closed ideal generated by commutators is all of $D$; otherwise a nonzero unital commutative quotient would supply a character. Decomposing elements into selfadjoint parts and rescaling, choose selfadjoint contractions $s_i,t_i$ and elements $v_i,w_i$, for $1\leq i\leq m$, such that $$\label{eq:commutator}
 \left\|1-\sum_{i=1}^m v_i[s_i,t_i]w_i\right\|<\tfrac12.$$ Put $s_0=1$, $k=m+1$, and $M=\sum_i\|v_i\|\|w_i\|>0$. Under any nonzero unital representation $\sigma$, some represented commutator has norm greater than $\delta=(2M)^{-1}$. Since $$\|[S,T]\|\leq2\|S-c1\|\|T\|
 \leq\operatorname{diam}(\operatorname{sp}S)$$ when $S=S^*$, $\|T\|\leq1$, and $c$ is the midpoint of $\operatorname{sp}S$, the spectrum of some $\sigma(s_i)$ has diameter at least $\delta$.

<a id="eq:angle"></a>
There are pure states $\rho^0,\rho^1$ of $\sigma(D)$ taking that element to its two spectral endpoints. Indeed, a state attaining an endpoint extends from the unital abelian algebra $C^*(1,\sigma(s_i))$, and the states attaining that endpoint form a nonempty weak-\* compact face of the state space. An extreme point of this face is pure. The associated real expectation vectors $$v^\alpha=\big(\rho^\alpha(\sigma(s_b))\big)_{b=0}^m
 \in\{1\}\times[-1,1]^m\qquad(\alpha=0,1)$$ differ by at least $\delta$ in one coordinate. The set of pairs in $\big(\{1\}\times[-1,1]^m\big)^2$ with $\|v^0-v^1\|_\infty\geq\delta$ is compact. Consequently there is $0<r<1$, independent of $\sigma$, such that $$\label{eq:angle}
 \left|\left\langle\frac{v^0}{\|v^0\|},\frac{v^1}{\|v^1\|}\right\rangle\right|\leq r.$$ To justify the strict bound, two vectors in this compact set cannot be negatively collinear because both first coordinates are one; positive collinearity forces them to coincide, contrary to their separation.

<a id="eq:h"></a>
<a id="eq:p"></a>
Choose $G,n$ from Lemma <a href="#lem:gaussian" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:gaussian">36</a> for $k,r$ and form the selfadjoint element $$\label{eq:h}
 h=\sum_{b_1,\ldots,b_n=0}^m G_{b_1\ldots b_n}
 s_{b_1}\otimes\cdots\otimes s_{b_n}
 \in D^{\otimes_{\max}n}.$$ Let $\pi$ be any nonzero irreducible representation of this tensor product, with factor restrictions $\pi_j:D\to\mathcal B(H)$. For each $j$, choose the two pure states of $\pi_j(D)$ described above. By <a href="#eq:angle" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:angle">[eq:angle]</a> and Lemma <a href="#lem:gaussian" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:gaussian">36</a>, choosing one state $\rho_j$ from each pair makes $$\label{eq:p}
 p=\sum_{b_1,\ldots,b_n=0}^mG_{b_1\ldots b_n}
 \prod_{j=1}^n\rho_j(\pi_j(s_{b_j}))$$ strictly positive for one choice and strictly negative for another. Removing the normalizations multiplies each tensor evaluation by a positive number and so preserves its sign.

<a id="eq:compression"></a>
The von Neumann algebras $M_j=\pi_j(D)''$ are mutually commuting factors: a central element of any $M_j$ commutes with every factor image and hence with the irreducible joint representation, so is scalar. For one of the choices in <a href="#eq:p" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:p">[eq:p]</a>, use Lemma <a href="#lem:excision" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:excision">38</a> to choose positive norm-one elements $e_j\in\pi_j(D)$ with $$\|e_j\pi_j(s_b)e_j-\rho_j(\pi_j(s_b))e_j^2\|<\varepsilon
 \quad(0\leq b\leq m).$$ The product $e=\prod_j e_j$ is positive and has norm one by Lemma <a href="#lem:factors" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:factors">37</a>. Commuting distinct factors and telescoping their products in <a href="#eq:h" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:h">[eq:h]</a> yields $$\label{eq:compression}
 \|e\pi(h)e-pe^2\|
 \leq n\varepsilon\sum_{b_1,\ldots,b_n}|G_{b_1\ldots b_n}|.$$ All factors in this estimate are contractions.

If $p>0$ and $\pi(h)\leq0$, then $pe^2-e\pi(h)e\geq pe^2$, so the left side of <a href="#eq:compression" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:compression">[eq:compression]</a> is at least $p$. Choosing $\varepsilon$ sufficiently small is a contradiction. The negative choice of $p$, with its own excisers, similarly rules out $\pi(h)\geq0$. Thus $$\pi(h_+)\ne0,\qquad\pi((-h)_+)\ne0$$ for every nonzero irreducible $\pi$. Each positive part is full: a proper closed ideal containing it would yield a nonzero quotient and hence an irreducible representation killing it. The two positive parts are orthogonal by functional calculus. ◻

</details>

The canonical unital map from the finite tensor power into the infinite maximal tensor power preserves fullness and orthogonality. Thus Theorem <a href="#thm:tensor-intro" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:tensor-intro">4</a> also gives the affirmative answer to <a id="pm-cite-65"></a>[[KR](#ref-KR), Question 6.2].

<a id="sec:central"></a>
### Central sequence algebras

We now transfer the full signs of Theorem <a href="#thm:tensor-intro" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:tensor-intro">4</a> to the central sequence algebra and apply the absorption criterion of Robert and Tikuisis. This completes the implication from finite nuclear dimension to absorption.

Retain the free ultrafilter $\omega$ on $\mathbb N$. For a $C^*$-algebra $A$, let $$A_\omega=\ell^\infty(A)/\{(a_l):\lim_{l\to\omega}\|a_l\|=0\},\qquad
 F(A)=(A_\omega\cap A')/\operatorname{Ann}(A),$$ where $A$ is embedded diagonally and $$\operatorname{Ann}(A)=\{b\in A_\omega:ba=ab=0\text{ for every }a\in A\}.$$ For nonzero separable $A$, this quotient is nonzero and unital. If $(e_l)$ is a sequential positive contractive approximate identity, its class is central and represents the unit. Indeed, for a bounded central sequence $(b_l)$ and $a\in A$, commuting $b_l$ past $a$ shows $(e_lb_l-b_l)a\to_\omega0$, and likewise on the other side. Also $e_la\to a$, so the unit is nonzero.

We use the following two results.

<a id="stmt-thm-imports"></a>
<div id="thm:imports" class="theorem">

**Theorem 39** (Winter–Zacharias and Robert–Tikuisis). *Let $A\ne0$ be separable with $\dim_{\mathrm{nuc}}A\leq d<\infty$.*

1.  *If no hereditary subalgebra of $A$ has a finite-dimensional irreducible representation, then there are finitely many finite-dimensional algebras $E_j$, completely positive order-zero maps $\varphi_j:E_j\to F(A)$, and elements $c_j\in F(A)$ such that every matrix summand of every $E_j$ has size at least two and $$1_{F(A)}=\sum_jc_j^*\varphi_j(1_{E_j})c_j.$$ This is the matrix-threshold-two consequence of the covering-number bound $\operatorname{cov}(F(A))\le(d+1)^2$ <a id="pm-cite-66"></a>[[WZNuclear](#ref-WZNuclear), Definition 4.1 and Proposition 4.3].*

2.  *If $F(A)$ contains two full orthogonal positive elements, then $A\cong A\otimes_{\min}\mathcal Z$ <a id="pm-cite-67"></a>[[RT](#ref-RT), Theorem 1.2].*

*Both assertions apply to nonunital $A$, with the annihilator quotient just defined.*

</div>

<a id="stmt-lem-nocharacters"></a>
<div id="lem:nocharacters" class="lemma">

**Lemma 40**. *If $A\ne0$ is separable, nowhere scattered, and has finite nuclear dimension, then $F(A)$ has no characters.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-nocharacters"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-nocharacters</summary>

*Proof.* A hereditary subalgebra $H$ is strongly Morita equivalent to the ideal $I$ it generates. If $H$ had a matrix quotient, ideal correspondence would give an ideal $J\subseteq I$ for which $I/J$ is strongly Morita equivalent to a matrix algebra, hence to $\mathbb C$. By imprimitivity, $I/J$ would be elementary. Ideals of $I$ are ideals of $A$, contradicting nowhere scatteredness. This is also the equivalence in <a id="pm-cite-68"></a>[[TV](#ref-TV), Theorem 3.1].

Apply Theorem <a href="#thm:imports" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:imports">39</a>(1) to $A$, put $B=F(A)$, and retain its maps $\varphi_j:E_j\to B$, elements $c_j$, and displayed identity.

If $\chi:B\to\mathbb C$ were a character, its compositions with these maps would be order-zero positive functionals. Such a functional on $M_r$, $r\geq2$, is zero. To see this, a nonzero positive functional has a positive density matrix with an eigenvector $\xi$ of positive eigenvalue. For a unit eigenvector $\eta\perp\xi$, the orthogonal rank-one projections onto $(\xi+\eta)/\sqrt2$ and $(\xi-\eta)/\sqrt2$ both have strictly positive values, contrary to order zero. Thus $\chi\circ\varphi_j=0$ for every $j$, contradicting the displayed identity and $\chi(1_B)=1$. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-lem-reindex"></a>
<div id="lem:reindex" class="lemma">

**Lemma 41** (Commuting reindexed copies). *Let $A\ne0$ be separable, and let $D\subseteq F(A)$ be a separable unital subalgebra with the same unit. For every finite $n$, there is a unital $*$-homomorphism $$D^{\otimes_{\max}n}\longrightarrow F(A).$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-reindex"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-reindex</summary>

*Proof.* It suffices to prove that for every separable subalgebra $C\subseteq F(A)$, there is a unital $*$-homomorphism from $D$ into $F(A)$ with range commuting with $C$. This is Kirchberg’s reindexing mechanism, in the form recorded in <a id="pm-cite-69"></a>[[KR](#ref-KR), Proposition 2.2 and Corollary 2.3] for unital $A$. We include the annihilator details needed for nonunital $A$.

<a id="eq:liftbound"></a>
Choose a countable dense unital $*$-algebra $D_0\subset D$ over $\mathbb Q+i\mathbb Q$. For each $x\in D_0$, choose a bounded lift $(a_l^x)_l$ in $A$ that represents a central element mapping to $x$, with $$\label{eq:liftbound}
 \sup_l\|a_l^x\|\leq2\|x\|.$$ Take the zero lift for zero and the approximate identity for $1$. The quotient norm permits these choices: first lift within a bound strictly below $2\|x\|$, then truncate coordinate norms without changing the ultrapower class. The additive, scalar, multiplicative, and adjoint relation defects between these lifts annihilate $A$ on both sides along $\omega$.

Choose a countable dense family in $C$ with bounded lifts $(b_s^u)_s$ in $A$. Also fix a countable dense subset of $A$. List the following countably many requirements on an old index $l$: centrality of each $a_l^x$ against that subset; left and right products of every relation defect with that subset tending to zero; and $$a_l^1a\longrightarrow a,\qquad aa_l^1\longrightarrow a$$ for its elements $a$. All these requirements hold along $\omega$.

For each output index $s$, choose $l(s)$ so that the first $s$ requirements hold with error less than $1/s$, and also $$\|[a_{l(s)}^x,b_s^u]\|<1/s$$ for the first $s$ pairs $(x,u)$ in an enumeration. For this fixed $s$, the finitely many $b_s^u$ are fixed elements of $A$. Original centrality therefore makes all these commutator conditions $\omega$-large as conditions on $l$. A finite intersection with the other $\omega$-large conditions is nonempty, so the choice is possible.

The new sequences $(a_{l(s)}^x)_s$ are central, and all their relation defects annihilate $A$ on both sides: the tests hold in the ordinary limit, and the uniform bounds extend them from the dense subset to all of $A$. Thus they define a unital rational-complex $*$-homomorphism on $D_0$ into $F(A)$. The unit assertion follows from the approximate-identity tests. Bound <a href="#eq:liftbound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:liftbound">[eq:liftbound]</a> permits continuous extension to a complex unital $*$-homomorphism on $D$. Its range commutes with the dense family in $C$, hence with $C$. Injectivity is not needed.

Starting with one copy and repeatedly taking $C$ to be generated by the previous images gives $n$ commuting unital images of $D$. The universal property of the maximal tensor product gives the stated homomorphism. ◻

</details>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-reindex-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-reindex</summary>

*Proof of Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">[thm:main]</a>.* The assertion is immediate for $A=0$, so assume $A\ne0$. Assume first that $\dim_{\mathrm{nuc}}(A)<\infty$; we prove (1)$\Rightarrow$(2). By Lemma <a href="#lem:nocharacters" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:nocharacters">40</a>, $B=F(A)$ has no characters. Choose in $B$ a finite commutator witness as in <a href="#eq:commutator" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:commutator">[eq:commutator]</a>, and let $D$ be the unital $C^*$-subalgebra generated by all its entries. Then $D$ is separable and has no characters, since applying a character of $D$ to that inequality would give $1<1/2$.

By Theorem <a href="#thm:tensor-intro" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:tensor-intro">4</a>, some $D^{\otimes_{\max}n}$ contains full orthogonal positive elements $a,b$. Lemma <a href="#lem:reindex" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:reindex">41</a> supplies a unital homomorphism into $B$. Their images remain orthogonal and full: applying a unital homomorphism to finite ideal combinations approximating the unit proves that fullness passes to the target. Theorem <a href="#thm:imports" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:imports">39</a>(2) now gives $A\cong A\otimes_{\min}\mathcal Z$. Conversely, for separable nuclear $A$, absorption and Theorem <a href="#intro:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="intro:main">3</a> imply $\dim_{\mathrm{nuc}}(A)\le1$. This bound implies finite nuclear dimension, completing all three equivalences in Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">[thm:main]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-auto-remark-2"></a>
<div class="remark">

*Remark 42*. The same argument proves that $F(A)$ has two full orthogonal positive elements whenever $A\ne0$ is separable and $F(A)$ has no characters. Finite nuclear dimension enters the proof of Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">[thm:main]</a> only through the two imported results in Theorem <a href="#thm:imports" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:imports">39</a>.

</div>

<a id="subsec:cu-consequence"></a>
#### Cuntz-semigroup consequence

We use the completed stabilized Cuntz semigroup $\mathrm{Cu}(X)$ formed from $(X\otimes\mathcal K)_+$, with no distinguished scale. Isomorphisms in $\mathbf{Cu}$ preserve zero, addition, order, increasing sequential suprema, and the way-below relation in both directions. The isomorphism in Corollary <a href="#cor:regularity-cu" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cor:regularity-cu">2</a><span class="upright">(ii)</span> need not agree with $\mathrm{Cu}(\iota_A)$.

<details id="proof-stmt-auto-remark-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-auto-remark-2</summary>

*Proof of Corollary <a href="#cor:regularity-cu" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cor:regularity-cu">2</a>.* Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">[thm:main]</a> gives the equivalence of its three conditions. The companion <a id="pm-cite-70"></a>[[CuntzCompanion](#ref-CuntzCompanion), Corollaries 1.3 and 1.4] gives that <span class="upright">(i)</span> implies absorption and that <span class="upright">(ii)</span> is equivalent to absorption, even without the exclusion on elementary ideal subquotients.

To prove that absorption implies <span class="upright">(i)</span>, the case $A=0$ is immediate. Put $D=A\otimes\mathcal Z$ and $j_X(x)=x\otimes1_\mathcal Z$, so $j_A=\iota_A$. Strong self-absorption gives an isomorphism $\varphi:\mathcal Z\to\mathcal Z\otimes\mathcal Z$ approximately unitarily equivalent to $z\mapsto z\otimes1_\mathcal Z$. The companion’s nonunital calculation <a id="pm-cite-71"></a>[[CuntzCompanion](#ref-CuntzCompanion), proof of Corollary 1.4] gives $\mathrm{Cu}(j_D)=\mathrm{Cu}(\mathrm{id}_A\otimes\varphi)$, an isomorphism: the implementing unitaries $u_n\in\mathcal Z\otimes\mathcal Z$ act through $1_{M(A)}\otimes u_n\in M(A\otimes\mathcal Z\otimes\mathcal Z)$, and positive cuts and approximate units after stabilization give equality of the completed Cuntz maps.

For an absorption isomorphism $\theta:A\to D$, both sides of the first identity below send $a$ to $\theta(a)\otimes1_\mathcal Z$. Functoriality then gives $$\begin{aligned}
 (\theta\otimes\mathrm{id}_\mathcal Z)\circ j_A&=j_D\circ\theta,\\
 \mathrm{Cu}(j_A)&=\mathrm{Cu}(\theta\otimes\mathrm{id}_\mathcal Z)^{-1}
       \circ\mathrm{Cu}(j_D)\circ\mathrm{Cu}(\theta).
\end{aligned}$$ All three factors on the right are isomorphisms, proving <span class="upright">(i)</span>. Thus <span class="upright">(ii)</span> reaches the canonical condition <span class="upright">(i)</span> through absorption, not by identifying its abstract isomorphism with $\mathrm{Cu}(\iota_A)$. ◻

</details>

  "figures": [
    {
      "caption": "Dimension bound Absorption $A$ unital, separable, nuclear (later $A=\\widetilde{A_0}\\otimes\\mathcal Z$) $A$ separable, nowhere scattered,$dim_{nuc}(A) $⇓$ $⇓$ Localized matrix model; relative ranks and bounded witnesses Finite covering makes $F(A)$ characterless; choose separable characterless $D ⊆ F(A)$ with $1_{D} = 1_{F(A)}$ $⇓$ $⇓$ Supported commutant comparison; hereditary condition and weights $k$, $1 − k$ Independent splitting theorem: every nonzero unital characterless $D$ has full orthogonal signs in some $D^{⊗_{max}n}$ $⇓$ $⇓$ Two-colour corner approximation; stabilization and hereditary permanence Reindex commuting copies to obtain $D^{⊗_{max}n} → F(A)$ unital; the full signs pass to $F(A)$ $⇓$ $⇓$ $dim_{nuc}(A_{0}⊗𝒵) ≤ 1$ Robert–Tikuisis criterion: $A ≅ A ⊗ 𝒵$ The two proof branches. The left branch uses spatial tensor products, first constructs the unital model, and then passes to $A_{0} ⊗ 𝒵$ by hereditary permanence. The splitting theorem in the right column requires neither separability nor nuclearity of $D$; its application to $F(A)$ uses the displayed unital map. The branches meet only in the main equivalence.",
      "figure_id": "fig:proof",
      "pdf_page": 5,
      "source_evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "fig:proof",
          "exact_quote": "The two proof branches. The left branch uses spatial tensor\nproducts, first constructs the unital model, and then passes to\n$A_0\\otimes\\Z$ by hereditary permanence. The splitting theorem in the\nright column requires neither separability nor",

      ],
      "source_kind": "other",
      "tikz_pgf_source": null,
      "witness_description": "A flow diagram showing two parallel vertical columns connected by downward double arrows ($\\Downarrow$):\n\nLeft column:\n- Heading: **Dimension bound**\n- Node 1:\n  $A$ unital, separable, nuclear\n  (later $A = \\widetilde{A_0 \\otimes \\mathcal{Z}}$)\n- Downward arrow: $\\Downarrow$\n- Node 2:\n  Localized matrix model;\n  relative ranks and bounded witnesses\n- Downward arrow: $\\Downarrow$\n- Node 3:\n  Supported commutant comparison;\n  hereditary condition and weights $k$, $1 - k$\n- Downward arrow: $\\Downarrow$\n- Node 4:\n  Two-colour corner approximation;\n  stabilization and hereditary permanence\n- Downward arrow: $\\Downarrow$\n- Node 5:\n  $\\dim_{\\mathrm{nuc}}(A_0 \\otimes \\mathcal{Z}) \\le 1$\n\nRight column:\n- Heading: **Absorption**\n- Node 1:\n  $A$ separable, nowhere scattered,\n  $\\dim_{\\mathrm{nuc}}(A) < \\infty$\n- Downward arrow: $\\Downarrow$\n- Node 2:\n  Finite covering makes $F(A)$ characterless;\n  choose separable characterless $D \\subseteq F(A)$\n  with $1_D = 1_{F(A)}$\n- Downward arrow: $\\Downarrow$\n- Node 3:\n  Independent splitting theorem:\n  every nonzero unital characterless $D$ has\n  full orthogonal signs in some $D^{\\otimes_{\\max} n}$\n- Downward arrow: $\\Downarrow$\n- Node 4:\n  Reindex commuting copies to obtain\n  $D^{\\otimes_{\\max} n} \\longrightarrow F(A)$ unital;\n  the full signs pass to $F(A)$\n- Downward arrow: $\\Downarrow$\n- Node 5:\n  Robert–Tikuisis criterion:\n  $A \\cong A \\otimes \\mathcal{Z}$"
    }
